        function gptClean(gptText) {
            if (typeof (gptText) == "string") {
                gptText = gptText.replace(/\&/g, '%26');
                return gptText.replace(/\^|\*|#|\$|\"|\'|\=|\,|\!|\+|\~|\;|\(|\)|\<|\>|\[|\]|\?|\%|\:|\@|\;|\&|\||\{|\}|\\|/g, '');
            }
            return "blank";
        }

        const segments = identities
            .filter(identity => identity.type === 'AudienceSegmentsUser')
            .map(identity => identity.name)
            .filter(name => name);

        googletag.pubads()
            .setTargeting('property', 'value-of-property')    // Change and duplicate this line for as many additional keywords to send
            .setTargeting('doi', gptClean('10.1002/advs.202521507')) // Publication DOI
            .setTargeting('journalDoi', gptClean('10.1002/(ISSN)2198-3844')) // journal DOI
            .setTargeting('journalCode', gptClean('21983844')) // journal code
            .setTargeting('pbPage', gptClean(gptPage))
            .setTargeting('pbSubPage', gptClean(gptSubPage))
            .setTargeting('AudienceSegmentsUser', segments)
            .setTargeting('urlpath', gptClean(document.location.pathname))
			.setTargeting('env', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.site?.environment || 'n/a');


        googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-leaderboard')
            .defineSizeMapping(sizes.leaderboard)

                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

<!-- loading Javascript for using the persistence Service -->
    <script src="/wro/7eefe1ac3fa1fd1db62439ce305b72e4595ffa0f~thiss-ds.js" nonce="9efac11ed1e54e66-SJC"></script>

    <script type="text/javascript" nonce="9efac11ed1e54e66-SJC">

        function getParameter(parameterName) {
            let result = null,
                tmp = [];
            location.search
                .substr(1)
                .split("&")
                .forEach(function (item) {
                    tmp = item.split("=");
                    if (tmp[0] === parameterName) result = tmp[1];
                });
            return result;
        }

        var SeamlessAccessService = {
            spUrl: "/action/ssostart",
            returnURL: window.location.href,
            my_context: 'seamlessaccess.org',
            queryParamsDelimiter: encodeURIComponent('?'),
            _persistenceService: null,
            _getPersistenceService: function () {
                if (!this._persistenceService) {
                    this._persistenceService = new thiss.PersistenceService('https://service.seamlessaccess.org/ps/');
                }
                return this._persistenceService;
            },

            getWAYFLessUrl: function (entityId) {
                let redirectURL = getParameter('redirectUri');
                if(redirectURL == null){
                    redirectURL = encodeURIComponent(this.returnURL);
                }
                if(redirectURL.indexOf('saml_referrer') < 0){
                    redirectURL += encodeURIComponent(((redirectURL.indexOf(this.queryParamsDelimiter) !== -1) ? '&' : '?') + 'saml_referrer');
                }
                return this.spUrl + "?idp=" + encodeURIComponent(entityId)
                    + "&redirectUri=" + redirectURL;
            },
            retrieveEntity: function () {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.entities(self.my_context)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // get the results
                            var results = res.data;
                            return resolve(results);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            reject('retrieve entity error: ' + err);
                        });
                });
            },
            updateEntity: function (entitydata) {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                var jsonObj = {};
                try {
                    //try to parse it, if it is a json string
                    jsonObj = JSON.parse(entitydata.val());
                } catch (e) {
                    jsonObj = entitydata;
                }
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.update(self.my_context, jsonObj)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // Process the results
                            var ssoInstitutions = res.data;
                            return resolve(ssoInstitutions);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to update the local storage duo to: ' + err);
                            reject(err);
                        });
                })
            },
            removeEntity: function (entityId) {
                var self = this;
                var persistService = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    persistService.remove(self.my_context, entityId)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            return resolve(res);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to remove from local storage duo to: ' + err);
                        });
                })
            },
            getAccessibleEntityStatus: function (doi, entityId, entityTitle) {
                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    $.ajax({
                        type: 'POST',
                        url: '/action/seamlessAccess',
                        accept: {
                            text: "application/json"
                        },
                        data: {doi: doi, entityid: entityId, entityTitle: entityTitle, eventGroupKey: '42df0783-97f7-4295-87e7-ddbcbf28031d'}

                    }).done(function (status) {
                        return resolve(status);
                    });
                });
            },
            getEntitiesWithAllInfo: function (doi) {
                let self = this,
                    arrayOfPromises = [];
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    self.retrieveEntity().then(function (res) {
                        let allSsoInstitutions = res;
                        for (let i in allSsoInstitutions) {
                            let ssoInstitution = allSsoInstitutions[i];
                            arrayOfPromises.push(new Promise(function (resolve) {
                                self.getAccessibleEntityStatus(doi, ssoInstitution.entity.entityID, ssoInstitution.entity.title)
                                    .then(function (entityStatus) {
                                        resolve({
                                            entityID: ssoInstitution.entity.entityID,
                                            entityTitle: ssoInstitution.entity.title,
                                            entityStatusObj: entityStatus,
                                            entitySsoUrl: self.getWAYFLessUrl(ssoInstitution.entity.entityID)
                                        });
                                    }, function (err) {
                                        console.log('failed to get the entity status: ' + err);
                                    });
                            }));
                        }
                        Promise.all(arrayOfPromises)
                            .then(function (finalResponse) {
                                resolve(finalResponse);
                            });

                    }).catch(function (err) {
                        reject(err);
                    })
                });
            }
        };

        window.addEventListener('load', function () {
            document.querySelectorAll(".sso-institution").forEach(function (institution) {
                var entityId = institution.getAttribute('data-entityid');
                var entityName = institution.getAttribute('data-name');
                var entitydata = {
                    entityID: entityId,
                    title: entityName
                };
                institution.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
                    e.preventDefault();
                    var self = this;
                    SeamlessAccessService.updateEntity(entitydata).then(function (res) {
                        console.log(res);
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    }).catch(function (err){
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    });
                });
            });
        });

    </script>

        (function() {
            function riskifiedBeaconLoad() {
                var store_domain = 'https://advanced.onlinelibrary.wiley.com';
                var session_id = '4D8B1DBDDBD332A3B9A3ADB4F57ABA32';
                var url = ('https:' == document.location.protocol ? 'https://' : 'http://')
                    + "beacon.riskified.com?shop=" + store_domain + "&sid=" + session_id;
                var s = document.createElement('script');
                s.type = 'text/javascript';
                s.async = true;
                s.src = url;
                var x = document.getElementsByTagName('script')[0];
                x.parentNode.insertBefore(s, x);
            }
            if (window.attachEvent)
                window.attachEvent('onload', riskifiedBeaconLoad)
            else
                window.addEventListener('load', riskifiedBeaconLoad, false);
        })();
    </script>

   <div class="abstract-group ">
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" lang="en" data-lang="en" lang-name="English" id="section-1-en">
         <h2 id="d62439087" class="article-section__header section__title main abstractlang_en main">ABSTRACT</h2>
         <div class="article-section__content en main">
            
            <p>Lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) are proposed as an attractive non-viral alternative for mRNA-based CAR-T cell engineering. However, the impact of the LNP charge and the composition of the transfection medium remain to be fully explored. Here, charge-neutral C12-200 LNPs and cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs were compared for ex vivo transfection of activated primary human T cells. C12-200 LNPs achieved efficient transfection with high cell viability in serum-free medium supplemented with apolipoprotein E. Transfection efficiency correlated with low-density lipoprotein receptor expression following T cell activation, suggesting receptor-mediated uptake. In contrast, cationic LNPs demonstrated superior mRNA expression independent of medium composition or T cell activation state, albeit with reduced cytocompatibility. Both LNPs efficiently delivered anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA into primary T cells, enabling potent cytotoxicity against CD20<sup>+</sup> Raji cells in vitro. Together, our findings demonstrate that LNP charge, selected proteins in the culture medium, and T cell activation collectively play a critical role in ex vivo T cell engineering. Here, cationic LNPs offer the advantage of unspecific charge-dependent transfection, inducing high mRNA expression independent on T cell activation status or complex cell culture media. These traits offer opportunities to extend expression kinetics, transfect less activated T cell phenotypes, and simplify CAR-T cell manufacturing.</p>
            </div>
      </section>
   </div>
   <div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="below-abstract-group"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div>
   
   <section class="article-section article-section__full">
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0010">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="advs74482-sec-0010-title">1 Introduction</h2>
         
         <p>Rapid improvements in chimeric antigen receptor (CAR)-T cell therapy for hematological malignancies have significantly contributed to establishing immunotherapy as a principal cancer treatment [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0001" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0001_R_d62438935e443" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 1 - Science">1</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0002" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0002_R_d62438935e443" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2 - Frontiers in Chemistry">2</a></span>]. As of July 2025, seven CAR-T cell therapies have been approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for the treatment of B-cell lymphomas, acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL), and multiple myeloma [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0003" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0003_R_d62438935e446" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 3 - Frontiers in Immunology">3</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0007" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0007_R_d62438935e446" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7">7</a></span>]. The most recent FDA-approved therapy, Aucatzyl (Obecabtagene autoleucel), has been granted conditional marketing authorization at the European Medicines Agency (EMA) for patients with B-ALL [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0008_R_d62438935e449" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8">8</a></span>]. All these therapies are autologous CAR-T cell products in which T cells are isolated from the patient, activated, and modified ex vivo to introduce the CAR receptor. The latter enables T cells to specifically recognize and eliminate tumor cells by HLA-independent targeting of tumor-associated antigens. Subsequently, the engineered T cells are expanded and re-infused into the patient in combination with lymphodepleting chemotherapy [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0009" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0009_R_d62438935e452" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Current Opinion in Biotechnology">9</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0010" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0010_R_d62438935e452" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 10 - Nature">10</a></span>]. Up until today, the approved CAR-T cell therapies have been produced using lentiviral or gammaretroviral vectors [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0011_R_d62438935e455" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Frontiers in Immunology">11</a></span>]. Such vectors allow for stable integration of the CAR transgene into the T cell genome, leading to long-term CAR expression [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0012" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0012_R_d62438935e459" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - Signal transduction and targeted therapy">12</a></span>]. Nevertheless, the use of viral vectors entails important drawbacks such as limited packaging capacity, inconsistent transduction yields, and high manufacturing costs [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0011_R_d62438935e462" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Frontiers in Immunology">11</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0013" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0013_R_d62438935e462" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 13 - Cytotherapy">13</a></span>]. In addition, GMP production of viral capsids is labor-intensive and difficult to scale up with long lead times due to limited production capacity, which results in a further increase of production costs and a decrease in accessibility of personalized CAR-T cell treatment [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0011_R_d62438935e465" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Frontiers in Immunology">11</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0014_R_d62438935e465" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - International Journal of Nanomedicine">14</a></span>].</p>
         
         <p>To address these challenges, non-viral transfection strategies constitute an attractive alternative [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0015_R_d62438935e471" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Biotechnology Advances">15</a></span>]. Electroporation, a state-of-the-art physical delivery technology, enables the intracellular delivery of large payloads [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0016" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0016_R_d62438935e474" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 16 - Biotechnology Progress">16</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0017_R_d62438935e474" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - Journal of Nanobiotechnology">17</a></span>]. However, its use is hampered by significant cytotoxicity and undesired variance in gene expression and T cell functionality [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0018_R_d62438935e477" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Nano Letters">18</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0019_R_d62438935e477" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 19 - Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences">19</a></span>]. Therefore, more gentle non-viral transfection methods, including lipid nanoparticles (LNPs), are of current interest for T cell engineering, as they provide several advantages [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0015_R_d62438935e480" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Biotechnology Advances">15</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0020" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0020_R_d62438935e480" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 20 - Advanced Drug Delivery Reviews">20</a></span>]. LNPs allow for a variety of cargoes to be delivered in cells, including mRNA, pDNA, and proteins such as CRISPR-Cas9 ribonucleoprotein (RNP) complexes, without affecting T cell viability or inducing marked phenotypic alterations [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0014_R_d62438935e483" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - International Journal of Nanomedicine">14</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0021_R_d62438935e483" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 21 - European Journal of Pharmaceutics and Biopharmaceutics">21</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0025" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0025_R_d62438935e483" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 25 - Journal of Biomedical Materials Research Part A">25</a></span>]. In addition, LNPs represent a robust and easily scalable nucleic acid delivery platform, which allows to produce more cost-effective therapies compared to viral vectors [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0011_R_d62438935e487" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Frontiers in Immunology">11</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0026" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0026_R_d62438935e487" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 26 - Nano Letters">26</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0027_R_d62438935e490" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 27 - Nature Reviews Materials">27</a></span>].</p>
         
         <p>The non-viral introduction of CAR receptors in T cells can be obtained using diverse engineering methods, which include both integrating approaches, such as CRISPR or transposons, and non-integrating strategies, like plasmid DNA (pDNA) or mRNA encoding for a CAR [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0014_R_d62438935e496" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - International Journal of Nanomedicine">14</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0028" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0028_R_d62438935e496" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 28 - Journal for ImmunoTherapy of Cancer">28</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0029" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0029_R_d62438935e499" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 29 - Frontiers in Immunology">29</a></span>]. Specifically, mRNA allows a transient CAR expression, offering multiple advantages. First, rapid and easy production of mRNA significantly reduces costs compared to viral vectors [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0030_R_d62438935e502" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 30 - Molecular Therapy">30</a></span>]. Second, due to its transient nature, the risk for on-target off-tumor toxicity can be mitigated as the CAR-T cells will not persist for a prolonged time in the patient [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0024_R_d62438935e505" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Molecular Therapy Methods &amp; Clinical Development">24</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0030_R_d62438935e505" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 30 - Molecular Therapy">30</a></span>]. In principle, transient mRNA CAR-T cells enable repeated dosing strategies that may enhance functionality. Unlike stably engineered lentiviral CAR-T cells, which become progressively hypofunctional under prolonged tumor microenvironment exposure, freshly dosed transient CAR-T cells could avoid exhaustion and improve efficacy [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0031_R_d62438935e508" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 31 - Nature Reviews Clinical Oncology">31</a></span>]. On the other hand, this approach would require the availability of sufficient patient-derived T cells and a significant reduction in current manufacturing costs to ensure feasibility. In addition, LNPs potentially enable co-delivery of various mRNAs, encoding different receptors, each targeting distinct tumor-associated antigens. As heterogeneity in tumor tissue remains a formidable challenge, the prospect of simultaneously targeting multiple tumor antigens is a major advantage [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0030_R_d62438935e512" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 30 - Molecular Therapy">30</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0032" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0032_R_d62438935e512" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 32 - Nature Communications">32</a></span>].</p>
         
         <p>Nevertheless, T cells remain notoriously hard-to-transfect [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0033" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0033_R_d62438935e518" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 33 - Biomaterials">33</a></span>]. Libraries of diverse charge-neutral LNP compositions, mainly focusing on distinct ionizable lipid designs, have been screened for ex vivo T cell engineering, albeit with varying success [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0018_R_d62438935e521" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Nano Letters">18</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0024_R_d62438935e521" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Molecular Therapy Methods &amp; Clinical Development">24</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0034" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0034_R_d62438935e524" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 34 - Nano Letters">34</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0035" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0035_R_d62438935e524" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 35 - ACS Biomaterials Science &amp; Engineering">35</a></span>]. Of note, Cheng et&nbsp;al. developed Selective Organ Targeting (SORT) LNPs, for example, by additionally incorporating a permanently charged cationic lipid, to mediate tissue-specific RNA delivery in vivo [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0036" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0036_R_d62438935e527" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 36 - Nature Nanotechnology">36</a></span>]. Positively charged LNPs may offer advantages for ex vivo T cell transfection as well, by enhancing electrostatic interactions with the T cell membrane and facilitating endocytosis. However, such LNP formulations have not been explored for this purpose to date. Together with the LNP surface charge, the composition of the transfection medium may have a profound impact on T cell binding and transfection efficiency. For instance, apolipoprotein E (ApoE) adsorption to the surface of charge-neutral, but not cationic, LNPs was shown to facilitate low-density lipoprotein (LDL) receptor-mediated RNA delivery into hepatocytes following intravenous administration [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0037" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0037_R_d62438935e530" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 37 - Neurology and therapy">37</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0040_R_d62438935e530" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 40 - Molecular Therapy">40</a></span>]. Likewise, Dilliard et&nbsp;al. demonstrated that LNP charge dictates the preferential adsorption of well-defined serum proteins in vivo, which influences binding to and transfection of specific cell types [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0038" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0038_R_d62438935e534" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 38 - Journal of Controlled Release">38</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0039" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0039_R_d62438935e534" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 39 - Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences">39</a></span>]. For ex vivo T cell engineering, given the variability and safety concerns associated with serum, the use of chemically defined serum-free medium supplemented with specific recombinant proteins is favored [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0041" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0041_R_d62438935e537" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 41 - Frontiers in Immunology">41</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0043" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0043_R_d62438935e537" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 43 - Cell and Gene Therapy Insights">43</a></span>]. It is conceivable that such proteins likewise interact with the LNP surface, which could influence T cell binding. Nevertheless, the interplay between LNP charge and transfection medium composition and its impact on the efficacy of ex vivo T cell engineering remains unclear to date.</p>
         
         <p>It is well-known that T cells upregulate both the LDL and transferrin receptor upon activation. This study aims to investigate how LNP charge (i.e., LNPs modified with the permanently charged cationic lipid DOTAP) and medium composition (i.e., chemically defined T cell culture medium spiked with ApoE or transferrin) affect cell uptake and mRNA-based nanoCAR engineering of primary human T cells as a function of time post-activation. Our data reveals that the LNP charge defines the need for distinct transfection conditions to achieve optimal T cell engineering for the manufacturing of functional nanoCAR-T cells ex vivo.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0020">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="advs74482-sec-0020-title">2 Materials and Methods</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0030">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0030-title">2.1 Cell Lines, Primary Human T Cells, and Culture Conditions</h3>
            
            <p>Jurkat E6.1 cells (ATCC no. TIB-152, Research resource identification (RRID): CVCL_0367), an immortalized T lymphoblast cell line, were obtained from American Type Culture Collection and cultured in Roswell Park Memorial Institute (RPMI) 1640 medium (Gibco, Thermo Fisher Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA) supplemented with 10% fetal bovine serum (FBS, Biowest, Nuaillé, France), 100 U/mL penicillin, 100&nbsp;µg/mL streptomycin (P/S, Gibco, Merelbeke, Belgium) and 2&nbsp;mg/mL L-glutamine (Sigma–Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA). Mycoplasma contamination was assessed using the MycoAlert Mycoplasma detection kit (Lonza, Basel, Switzerland) according to the manufacturer's instructions, and all cell cultures were tested negative. Cell culture concentration was maintained between 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>5</sup> and 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells by adding fresh medium every 2 to 3 days, depending on the cell density. Prior to transfection, Jurkat cells were seeded at a concentration of 6&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/well in a 96-well-plate.</p>
            
            <p>Human buffy coats were obtained with informed consent from the Red Cross Flanders Biobank (Ghent, Belgium). The donor material was used following the guidelines of the Medical Ethical Committee of Ghent University Hospital (Ghent, Belgium). Peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) were isolated from buffy coats using density gradient centrifugation with Lymphoprep (Stem Cell Technologies, Vancouver, Canada). After centrifugation, the freshly isolated PBMCs were washed in Dulbecco's Phosphate Buffered Saline (DPBS, Sigma–Aldrich, St. Louis, MO, USA), and the remaining red blood cells were lysed by ACK Lysing buffer (Invitrogen). Human pan CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells were isolated by magnetic negative selection using the EasySep Human T Cell Isolation Kit (Stem Cell Technologies, Vancouver, Canada) according to the manufacturer's instructions. T cells were seeded at a final concentration of 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells/mL in Iscove's modified Dulbecco's medium (IMDM) GlutaMax (Gibco, Thermo Fisher Scientific, Waltham, MA, USA), supplemented with 10% FBS, 100 U/mL penicillin, and 100&nbsp;µg/mL streptomycin (complete IMDM, cIMDM). Depending on the performed experiment, T cells were activated using 10&nbsp;µL/mL per 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells/mL of ImmunoCult Human CD3/CD28/CD2 T Cell Activator (Stem Cell Technologies, Vancouver, Canada) and supplemented with 10&nbsp;ng/mL IL-2 (Human Recombinant IL-2, CHO-expressed, StemCell, Vancouver, Canada). Stimulated T cells were kept in culture for up to 10 days, and concentration was maintained between 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>5</sup> and 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells/mL by adding fresh cIMDM. Before transfection, T cells were seeded at 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/well in a 96-well-plate. Mycoplasma contamination of primary human T cells was assessed using the MycoAlert Mycoplasma detection kit (Lonza, Basel, Switzerland) according to the manufacturer's instructions, and all tested cultures were free of contamination.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0040">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0040-title">2.2 mRNA and IVT Production</h3>
            
            <p>CleanCap eGFP mRNA with 5-methoxyuridine (5moU), CleanCap firefly luciferase (Luc) mRNA (5moU) (Trilink Biotechnologies, San Diego, CA), or in vitro transcribed (IVT) anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA was used as specified in different experiments. The mRNA encoding for anti-CD20 nanoCAR was kindly provided by the lab of Prof. Bart Vandekerckhove (Faculty of Medicine and Health Sciences, Ghent University, Ghent, Belgium). The anti-CD20 nanoCAR construct was designed with a sequence encoding for CD20 nanobody combined with the CD8α hinge and transmembrane region, followed by the 4_1BBζ and CD3ζ intracellular signaling moieties [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0044_R_d62438935e588" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 44 - International Journal of Molecular Sciences">44</a></span>]. The IVT reaction, precipitation, and purification of the anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA were performed as described by Ingels et&nbsp;al. [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0045" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0045_R_d62438935e591" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 45 - Cytotherapy">45</a></span>]. Here, N1-methyl-pseudouridine-5’-triphosphate (ThermoFisher Scientific) and CleanCap AG (Trilink Biotechnologies, San Diego, CA) were used as nucleotide modification and capping reagents, respectively.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0050">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0050-title">2.3 LNP Formulation and Characterization</h3>
            
            <div class="paragraph-element">DOTAP (1,2-dioleoyl-3-trimethylammonium propane), cholesterol, DOPE (1,2-dioleoyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphoethanolamine), DMG-PEG 2000 (1,2-dimyristoyl-rac-glycero-3-methoxypolyethylene glycol-2000) were purchased from Avanti Polar Lipids (Alabaster, USA). C12-200 ionizable lipid was obtained from Corden Pharma (Plankstadt, Germany). Alternative ionizable lipids used were SM-102 and ALC-0315, purchased from BroadPharm (Cat. No. BP-25499 and BP-25498, respectively), and DLin-MC3-DMA (Cat. No. HY-112251, MedChemExpress). LNP formulations were prepared using the microfluidic mixing technique with a NanoAssemblr Spark (Precision Nanosystems, Vancouver, Canada). Briefly, lipid components (ionizable lipid, DOPE, cholesterol, DMG-PEG) were dissolved in ethanol at molar ratios of 50:10:38.5:1.5. For cell interaction studies, 0.5% to 1.2% DiD dye (1,1'-dioctadecyl-3,3,3',3'-tetramethylindodicarbocyanine, 4-chlorobenzenesulfonate salt, Invitrogen, Merelbeke, Belgium) was added to the lipid mixture. DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs contain an additional fifth lipid component with molar ratios of 50:8:23.3:1.3:17.5 (DOTAP:DOPE:cholesterol:DMG-PEG:C12-200). The aqueous phase was prepared by dissolving mRNA in sodium acetate (NaOAc) buffer (50&nbsp;m<span class="smallCaps">m</span>, pH 4). For all LNP formulations, the total amount of ionizable lipids was calculated to obtain a nitrogen-to-phosphate ratio (N/P) of 6, and the two phases were mixed at a 1:2 volume ratio (EtOH:aqueous). After formulation, LNPs were 1:1 diluted in PBS<sup>−/−</sup> (w/o Ca<sup>2+</sup> and Mg<sup>2+</sup>, pH 7.4) and stored at 4°C for a maximum of 2 weeks. A total final lipid concentration of 3.05 and 2.69&nbsp;mg/mL was obtained for C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, respectively. As quality control, the hydrodynamic diameter and polydispersity index (PDI) were determined using a Malvern Zetasizer nano-ZS (Malvern Instruments Ltd., Worcestershire, UK) at 25°C. LNPs were diluted 1/50 in HEPES buffer (20&nbsp;m<span class="smallCaps">m</span>, pH 7.4). The ζ-potential was measured with a Malvern Zetasizer nano-ZS (Malvern Instruments Ltd., Worcestershire, UK) at 25°C by using a folded capillary zeta cell (DTS1070). mRNA encapsulation efficiency (EE) and final concentration were quantified by the Quant-iT RiboGreen RNA Assay (Invitrogen, Merelbeke, Belgium) according to manufacturer's protocol.

               <div class="inline-equation" id="advs74482-disp-0001"><span class="inline-equation__construct"><span><!--FIGURE--><span class="fallback__mathEquation" data-altimg="/cms/asset/7f76aa68-3c72-43e8-bb05-8ccff367b88f/advs74482-math-0001.png"></span><math altimg="urn:x-wiley:21983844:media:advs74482:advs74482-math-0001" display="block" location="graphic/advs74482-math-0001.png">

            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0060">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0060-title">2.4 Quantification of LNP-Mediated mRNA Delivery in Jurkat Cell Line Using Flow Cytometry</h3>
            
            <p>Confluent Jurkat cells were counted using Trypan Blue exclusion dye (Merck, Darmstadt, Germany) and resuspended to a concentration of 1.2&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells/mL in the appropriate cell culture medium, specified for each experiment. The media used were RPMI 1640, X-VIVO 15 serum-free hematopoietic cell medium (Lonza, Cat. No. BE02-060F, Basel, Switzerland) or ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF) (Stem Cell, Cat. No. #10981, Vancouver, Canada). The latter two were spiked with additional Human Transferrin Protein (Acro Biosystems, Cat. No. TRN-H52H3, His Tag (MALS verified)) or Human Apolipoprotein E3 (Acro Biosystems, Cat. No. APE-H5246, His Tag) at a concentration of 1&nbsp;µg/mL (Table <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>). Cells were seeded at 6&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/well in 50&nbsp;µL medium in a flat-bottom 96-well-plate. LNPs were diluted in the corresponding cell culture medium (with spiked proteins) and added to the cells at a concentration of 1&nbsp;ng/µL mRNA based on the final encapsulated concentration determined with the Ribogreen Assay. The cells were incubated for 24&nbsp;h at 37°C with 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. Flow cytometry was performed to evaluate eGFP expression after 24&nbsp;h of incubation with the LNPs. Cells were transferred to a U-bottom 96-well-plate, centrifuged, and resuspended in 100&nbsp;µL flow buffer (PBS <sup>−/−</sup> with 1% BSA and 0.1% NaN<sub>3</sub>). Samples were measured using a CytoFLEX flow cytometry plate reader for 96-well-plates (Beckman Coulter, Krefeld, Germany) and CytExpert software (Version 2.1). eGFP protein and DiD dye were excited with 488 and 638&nbsp;nm lasers and detected with 525/40 and 660/20 filters, respectively. FlowJo software (Version 10.8.1, Treestar Inc.) was used to perform data analysis.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0070">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0070-title">2.5 Evaluation of LNP-Mediated mRNA Delivery in Activated Primary Human T Cells Using Flow Cytometry and Confocal Microscopy</h3>
            
            <p>Primary human CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells were isolated from PBMCs, activated, and kept in culture as previously described. On day 3, day 6, and day 10 post-activation, T cells were collected, counted, and resuspended at a concentration of 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>6</sup> cells/mL in IM-XF spiked with additional Human Transferrin Protein or Human Apolipoprotein E3 (ApoE) at a concentration of 1&nbsp;µg/mL. Cells were seeded at a cell density of 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/well in 50&nbsp;µL of medium in a flat bottom 96-well-plate. LNPs containing eGFP or Luc mRNA were diluted to a concentration of 2&nbsp;ng/µL in IM-XF with or without additional proteins. The diluted LNPs were added 1:1 to the seeded T cells, providing a final concentration of 1&nbsp;ng/µL mRNA and the cells were incubated for 24&nbsp;h at 37°C and 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. After 24&nbsp;h, detection of eGFP expression was performed as described above via flow cytometry. To correct for autofluorescence, each condition was normalized to the fluorescence of cells treated with Luc mRNA LNPs. For the determination of LNP-T cell interaction, transfected T cells were visualized with a Nikon A1R HD confocal laser scanning microscope (Nikon Benelux, Belgium) with a 60x water immersion lens (SR plan apo IR 60X WI, NA 1.3, WD 80&nbsp;µm). In detail, T cells were transfected with LNPs containing DiD lipid dye at a concentration of 1.2 mol%. After 24&nbsp;h incubation, the cells were stained with 1/2000 dilution of Hoechst 33342 (Invitrogen, Belgium) in PBS<sup>−/−</sup> for 10&nbsp;min at 37°C. Subsequently, the cells were washed and resuspended in IM-XF. 408 and 633&nbsp;nm laser lines were used to determine eGFP expression and the presence of DiD-LNPs in T cells. Further processing of the images was achieved using ImageJ software.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0080">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0080-title">2.6 Quantification of Cell Viability with CellTiter-Glo Assay</h3>
            
            <p>The CellTiter-Glo luminescent cell viability assay (Promega, Belgium) was used according to the manufacturer's protocol to determine cell viability after 24&nbsp;h incubation with LNPs. In this assay, the number of viable cells after LNP treatment can be determined based on quantitation of the ATP present, as an indicator of metabolically active cells. In brief, a volume of CellTiter-Glo reagent equal to the LNP-T cell culture was added directly to the cells and shaken on an orbital shaker (120&nbsp;rpm) at room-temperature to allow for cell lysis. After 10&nbsp;min, the cell lysates were transferred to an opaque flat-bottom 96-well-plate, and the plate was incubated for 10&nbsp;min at room-temperature to stabilize the luminescent signal. Next, luminescence was measured using a GloMax microplate reader (Promega, Belgium) with a detection wavelength of 350 to 650&nbsp;nm. To calculate cell viability, the luminescence of LNP-treated T cells was normalized to non-treated control samples that were exposed to the same activation protocol.</p>
            
            <div class="paragraph-element">To determine transfection yield, the cell viability data were combined with transfection efficiency measured by flow cytometry. This parameter represents the percentage of viable transfected cells of the initial cell population. The percentage transfection yield can be calculated by multiplying the percentage of eGFP-positive cells and the percentage of viable cells:

            </div>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0090">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0090-title">2.7 Analysis of Receptor Expression After T Cell Stimulation</h3>
            
            <p>To analyze the receptor expression and activation status of T cells after initial stimulation, cells were stimulated with ImmunoCult CD3/CD28/CD2 activator and kept in culture for up to 10 days. On day 0, 3, 6, and 10 post-activation&nbsp;cells were stained using the following anti-human monoclonal antibodies: CD25-APC (StemCell technologies, Vancouver, Canada), CD71-APC (Biolegend, USA), and LDLR-BV421 (BD biosciences). Briefly, cells were centrifuged at 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>5</sup> cells/100&nbsp;µL in a U-bottom 96-well-plate at 500&nbsp;g for 5&nbsp;min. Subsequently, the cells were resuspended in PBS<sup>−/−</sup> containing Human TruStain FcX (Biolegend, USA) at a concentration of 0.5% and incubated for 20&nbsp;min at room-temperature. Next, cells were washed in flow buffer (PBS<sup>−/−</sup> with 1% BSA and 0.1% NaN<sub>3</sub>) and incubated in flow buffer containing the indicated antibodies for 30&nbsp;min at 4°C. Sytox Green Nucleic Acid Stain (Invitrogen, Belgium) was used to distinguish between live and dead cell populations. After a washing step, cells were resuspended in flow buffer with 0.08% Sytox Green and analyzed with a MACSQuant Analyzer 16 (Miltenyi Biotec, Germany). Data analysis was performed using FlowJo software (Version 10.8.1, Treestar Inc.).</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0100">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0100-title">2.8 Optimization of Transfection Parameters for mRNA Delivery in Primary Human T Cells</h3>
            
            <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0110">
               
               <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section3" id="advs74482-sec-0110-title">2.8.1 mRNA Dose Titration</h4>
               
               <p>To assess the influence of mRNA dose on transfectability and toxicity, the same protocol as described above was used with some minor changes. Briefly, T cells were transfected in both IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE (1&nbsp;µg/mL). Transfections were performed on day 3 and 6 post-activation with a varying mRNA concentration of 0.5, 1, 2, and 3&nbsp;ng/µL mRNA to 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> T cells/100&nbsp;µL. After 24&nbsp;h incubation, eGFP expression was determined using flow cytometry, and cell viability was assessed by CellTiterGlo assay.</p>
               </section>
            
            <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0120">
               
               <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section3" id="advs74482-sec-0120-title">2.8.2 Evaluation of mRNA Kinetics in T Cells</h4>
               
               <p>The kinetics of mRNA expression after LNP-mediated delivery was assessed by transfection of activated T cells on day 3. Here, eGFP and Luc mRNA LNPs were added to 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/100&nbsp;µL at a concentration of 3&nbsp;ng/µL mRNA in both IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE (1&nbsp;µg/mL). Cells were incubated at 37°C and 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. After 24&nbsp;h, LNPs were removed by centrifugation (4&nbsp;min at 400&nbsp;g), and the cells were resuspended in fresh IM-XF with additional ImmunoCult Human CD3/CD28/CD2 T Cell Activator (1&nbsp;µL/mL). eGFP expression was evaluated after 24, 48, 72, and 96&nbsp;h post-transfection via flow cytometry. Luc mRNA LNPs were used to correct for autofluorescence during data analysis.</p>
               </section>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0130">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0130-title">2.9 nanoCAR Expression and Determination of Kinetics</h3>
            
            <p>Activated CD3<sup>+</sup> human T cells were transfected with C12-200 or DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs containing anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA at a concentration of 3&nbsp;ng/µL or 1&nbsp;ng/µL, respectively. Transfections were performed with 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells/100&nbsp;µL, and cells were resuspended in either IM-XF or IM-XF + ApoE (1&nbsp;µg/mL). The LNP-treated cells were incubated at 37°C and 5% CO<sub>2</sub>, and LNPs were removed after 24&nbsp;h. NanoCAR expression kinetics were determined after 24, 48, 72, and 96&nbsp;h post-transfection via flow cytometry. For analysis of nanoCAR expression on the surface, T cells were stained with an iFluor 488-conjugated MonoRab Rabbit Anti-Camelid VHH (Genscript by Bio-Connect, Huissen, The Netherlands). Briefly, cells were transferred to a U-bottom 96-well-plate and incubated with 0.5&nbsp;µL Human Trustain FcX (Biolegend, USA) in 100&nbsp;µL PBS<sup>−/−</sup> for 20&nbsp;min at room-temperature. Next, cells were washed in flow buffer and centrifuged. After removal of the supernatant, staining of the CAR receptors was performed in flow buffer using an antibody concentration of 0.5&nbsp;µL per 1&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>5</sup> cells/100&nbsp;µL, and the cells were incubated for 30&nbsp;min at 4°C. 7-AAD Viability Staining solution (Biolegend, USA) was added to the flow buffer before analysis to distinguish between live and dead cell populations. Samples were measured on a CytoFLEX flow cytometer (Beckman Coulter, Krefeld, Germany) and CytExpert software (Version 2.1). 7-AAD Viability dye and iFluor-488 Anti-Camelid VHH were excited with a 488&nbsp;nm laser and detected with 690/50 and 525/40 filters, respectively. Data analysis was performed using FlowJo software (Version 10.8.1, Treestar Inc.).</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0140">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0140-title">2.10 Tumor Cell Killing Efficiency of Anti-CD20 nanoCAR-T Cells</h3>
            
            <div class="paragraph-element">To evaluate the functionality of the transiently engineered anti-CD20 nanoCAR-T cells, an in vitro cytotoxicity assay was performed using luciferase-expressing CD20<sup>+</sup> Raji cells (Burkitts lymphoma). In detail, Raji cells (target cells, T) were plated in an opaque 96-well-plate at a density of 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>3</sup> cells/well in cIMDM. nanoCAR-T cells (effector cells, E) were cocultured at different ratios E:T (i.e., 40:1, 20:1, 10:1, 5:1, 1:1). The total transfected T cell population, consisting of engineered as well as non-transfected T cells, was used, resulting in identical total cell numbers for each specific E:T ratio. As a positive control, Raji cells incubated with 2% Triton X-100 were used to induce cell death. The plates were centrifuged and incubated at 37°C and 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. After 24&nbsp;h, 50&nbsp;µL of D-luciferin (0.3&nbsp;µg/µL in cIMDM) (IVISbrite D-Luciferin Potassium Salt Bioluminescent Substrate, Revvity, Belgium) was added to the cells and incubated for 10&nbsp;min at room-temperature. Subsequently, the luminescent signal was determined using the GloMax Discover microplate reader (Promega, Belgium). The percentage of lysis was calculated using the following formula, with background described as positive control (Raji + Triton):

         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0150">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0150-title">2.11 Statistical Analysis</h3>
            
            <p>Experiments were typically performed in technical triplicates with three or more biological repeats or independent donors. All data are presented as mean ± standard deviation (SD) unless otherwise stated. Statistical analyses were performed using GraphPad Prism v9.0.0 (La Jolla, USA). Two-way ANOVA with Tukey's multiple comparison test was used to compare transfection efficiencies across cell culture media in both Jurkat cells and primary human T cells. The same test was applied to assess statistical differences in mRNA dose titration experiments. Additional statistical tests are specified in the corresponding figure legends. A <i>p</i> value &lt;0.05 was considered statistically significant (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup> <i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; <sup>****</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001).</p>
            </section>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0160">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="advs74482-sec-0160-title">3 Results</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0170">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0170-title">3.1 LNP Charge and Cell Culture Medium Composition Affect Transfection Efficiency in Jurkat Cells</h3>
            
            <p>Initial screening experiments were performed to evaluate the influence of serum on LNP-mediated mRNA delivery in T cells. LNPs containing DLin-MC3-DMA (MC3), SM-102, or C12-200 ionizable lipids were formulated with enhanced green fluorescent protein (eGFP)-encoding mRNA at state-of-the-art molar ratios (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>). MC3 and SM-102 were included as clinically validated lipids, while C12-200 was selected for its previously reported efficiency in pDNA delivery to T cells [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0014_R_d62438935e1032" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - International Journal of Nanomedicine">14</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0027_R_d62438935e1032" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 27 - Nature Reviews Materials">27</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0046" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0046_R_d62438935e1035" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 46 - Journal of Controlled Release">46</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0047" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0047_R_d62438935e1035" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 47 - Nature Nanotechnology">47</a></span>]. Transfection efficiency was evaluated in Jurkat cells, an immortalized human T cell line commonly used in T cell studies, comparing serum-containing (RPMI 1640 + 10% FBS) and serum-free (X-VIVO) medium (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>). Only the C12-200 LNPs performed significantly better in the serum-free medium (32.5% eGFP+ cells), compared to the serum-containing condition (3.1% eGFP+ cells). Next, the impact of a permanently charged cationic lipid on the transfection efficiency of the C12-200 LNPs was evaluated by adding DOTAP as a fifth component to the quaternary LNP composition (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0001">1a</a>) [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0036" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0036_R_d62438935e1045" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 36 - Nature Nanotechnology">36</a></span>]. Both LNPs were formulated containing mRNA encoding for either eGFP or firefly luciferase (Luc), the latter used as a control to account for any background autofluorescence [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0048" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0048_R_d62438935e1048" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 48 - Scientific reports">48</a></span>]. An average hydrodynamic diameter of ∼120&nbsp;nm (<i>n</i> = 24) and ∼100&nbsp;nm (<i>n</i> = 12) was measured for C12-200 LNPs and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, respectively, with the latter typically showing elevated PDI values. As expected, the inclusion of DOTAP shifted the LNP's zeta-potential from near-neutral (-4.5 to 5.9&nbsp;mV) to a cationic charge at physiological pH (12.6 to 22.5&nbsp;mV) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0001">1b</a>). All LNP formulations showed a high mRNA encapsulation efficiency (Table <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a>). Transfection of Jurkat cells demonstrated that cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs markedly outperformed their charge-neutral counterparts in X-VIVO medium, achieving transfection efficiencies of 77.7 ± 7.5% (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 6) and 32.5 ± 19.3% (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3), respectively. However, when serum was present, both formulations only achieved very low mRNA transfection efficiency (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0001">1c,d</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0001"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/85b13f87-8eeb-46c5-8ef3-6e4e8bea80c2/advs74482-fig-0001-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/85b13f87-8eeb-46c5-8ef3-6e4e8bea80c2/advs74482-fig-0001-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/1e102168-4c27-44b2-a6e3-1e9595036c93/advs74482-fig-0001-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/85b13f87-8eeb-46c5-8ef3-6e4e8bea80c2/advs74482-fig-0001-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 1<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0001&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Physicochemical properties and Jurkat cell transfection of C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs in different cell culture media. (a) Composition of C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) lipid nanoparticle (LNP) formulations (mol%). (b) Hydrodynamic diameter (z-average, d.nm), polydispersity index (PDI), and zeta potential (mV) of C12-200 (blue) and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs (red), encapsulating enhanced green fluorescent protein (eGFP) or firefly luciferase (Luc) mRNA, respectively. Data points represent the mean ± SD of <i>n</i> independent formulations with C12-200 eGFP LNPs (<i>n</i> = 24), C12-200 Luc LNPs (<i>n</i> = 16), DOTAP(C12-200) eGFP mRNA LNPs (<i>n</i> = 12), and DOTAP(C12-200) Luc mRNA&nbsp;LNPs (<i>n</i> = 7), respectively. (c) Transfection efficiency and (d) fold mean fluorescence intensity (fMFI) of Jurkat cells after 24&nbsp;h incubation of 1&nbsp;ng/µL eGFP mRNA encapsulated in C12-200 or DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, in RPMI 1640 + 10% FBS (Fetal Bovine Serum) and X-VIVO, respectively. fMFI was calculated as MFI eGFP+ cells/MFI Luc CTR cells. Transfection data are represented as mean ± SD (C12-200 LNPs: <i>n</i> = 3, DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs: <i>n</i> = 6, biological replicates). Statistical analysis was performed using ordinary Two-way ANOVA with Tukey's multiple comparisons test (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; <sup>****</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001).</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>Having established that the composition of the cell culture medium is an important parameter, possibly through adsorption of proteins to the LNP surface, we next assessed LNP-mediated Jurkat cell transfection in two different serum-free T cell culture media (i.e., X-VIVO and ImmunoCult-XF T cell expansion medium (IM-XF)), spiked with either ApoE or transferrin (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2a</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a>). ApoE and transferrin can bind to the LDL receptor (LDL-R) and transferrin receptor (CD71), respectively, both of which are highly expressed on activated T cells and Jurkat cells [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0049" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0049_R_d62438935e1128" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 49 - Blood">49</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0051" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0051_R_d62438935e1128" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 51 - Journal of Lipid Research">51</a></span>]. In this way, directing the LNPs to these receptors via protein adsorption may lead to enhanced cellular uptake and transfection efficiency. Notably, cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs transfected Jurkat cells independent of medium composition, while conventional ionizable C12-200 LNPs specifically required the presence of ApoE in the cell culture medium (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2b</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2</a>). Also, the extent of mRNA expression per cell was significantly higher for C12-200 LNPs with additional ApoE (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2c</a>). In contrast, additional transferrin had no positive effect on the transfection properties of either LNP. These observations were mirrored in the cell interaction data, representing both LNPs bound to the cell membrane as well as internalized LNPs (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2d</a>). Analogous to transfection efficiency, T cell interaction was improved for C12-200 LNPs in combination with ApoE protein, while cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs bound to T cells equally well in all media. In addition, a cell viability above 65%, measured as metabolic activity using the CellTiter-Glo assay, indicated that the LNPs were relatively well tolerated (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2e</a>). As the quality of the final cell product in cancer immunotherapy is of main importance, the percentage of both viable and transfected cells (i.e., the yield) is a critical parameter. Acceptable yields of 50.5 ± 19.4% and 68.8 ±&nbsp;4.9% (mean ±&nbsp;SD, <i>n</i> = 2) were obtained for respectively C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF spiked with ApoE and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs in IM-XF without additional proteins (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0002">2f</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0002"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/95d935ec-a6dd-4323-8524-d547c55e5347/advs74482-fig-0002-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/95d935ec-a6dd-4323-8524-d547c55e5347/advs74482-fig-0002-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/52bb0237-3c4d-4b1f-bd76-ab2f5a8f118f/advs74482-fig-0002-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/95d935ec-a6dd-4323-8524-d547c55e5347/advs74482-fig-0002-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 2<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0002&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Transfection efficiency of Jurkat cells depends on LNP charge and cell culture medium composition. (a) Schematic overview of experimental procedure. Cells were transfected with either C12-200 or DOTAP(C12-200) lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) at a final mRNA concentration of 1&nbsp;ng/µL per 6&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> cells in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF), IM-XF + transferrin (1&nbsp;µg/mL), or IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL), respectively. Flow cytometry was used to determine eGFP expression after 24&nbsp;h incubation. Transfected cells are represented in green, dead cells in red, and non-transfected cells in grey. (b) Transfection efficiency and (c) fold mean fluorescence intensity (fMFI) of LNPs with or without the presence of additional extracellular proteins (i.e., transferrin and ApoE). fMFI was calculated as MFI eGFP+ cells/MFI Luc CTR cells. Data are represented as mean ± SD (<i>n</i> = 3, biological replicates). (e) Cell viability as determined by CellTiter-Glo assay after 24&nbsp;h incubation with LNPs. Data represent mean ± SD (<i>n</i> = 3, biological replicates). (f) Transfection yield, calculated by multiplying transfection efficiency (%) and cell viability (%), representing the percentage of viable transfected cells. Data show mean ± SD (<i>n</i> = 2, biological replicates). Statistical analysis was performed using ordinary Two-way ANOVA with Tukey's multiple comparisons test (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; <sup>****</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001). Illustration created in <a href="https://BioRender.com" class="linkBehavior">https://BioRender.com</a>.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0180">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0180-title">3.2 Cationic LNPs Achieve Robust Transfection of Primary T Cells Regardless of Media or Activation State</h3>
            
            <p>Given that primary human T cells better represent the physiological heterogeneity and activation states found in vivo, C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs were next tested in CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells to validate the findings from Jurkat cells. To this end, T cells were isolated from human buffy coats and subsequently activated using anti-CD3/CD28/CD2 antibodies in the presence of IL-2. Next, T cells were transfected on day 3, 6, or 10 post-activation to compare transfection efficiencies in T cells with different activation state (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3a</a>). The strong increase of CD25 (interleukin 2 receptor alpha chain), CD71 (transferrin receptor) and LDL expression clearly indicated successful T cell activation. However, the receptor density per cell markedly decreased on day 10 post-activation, suggesting a decline in T cell activation state (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>). Again, DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs transfected primary T cells equally well independent of the culture medium composition, while the performance of C12-200 LNPs clearly depended on ApoE, in line with the data obtained on Jurkat cells. Six days after activation, transfection efficiencies of up to 54.7 ± 6.6% and 35.1 ± 4.7% (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3) were obtained for respectively C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs in IM-XF with ApoE (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3b</a>). Of note, the extent of mRNA expression was noticeably higher for the cationic DOTAP-modified LNPs compared to their charge neutral counterparts (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3c</a>). Additionally, other conventional ionizable lipids (i.e., SM-102 and ALC-0315) were tested, albeit preliminary results on a single human donor suggest no improvement in transfection efficiency relative to the C12-200 formulation (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S4</a>). Replacement of cholesterol by β-sitosterol, a naturally occurring cholesterol analogue, was previously reported to improve transfection efficiency of NK cells by enhancing endosomal escape [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0052" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0052_R_d62438935e1230" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 52 - Journal of Controlled Release">52</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0053" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0053_R_d62438935e1230" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 53 - Nature Communications">53</a></span>]. However, our data suggest that inclusion of β-sitosterol in C12-200 LNPs does not further promote mRNA expression in primary T cells (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S5</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0003"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/831207e0-ce1f-490a-b8f1-42e5445f86ef/advs74482-fig-0003-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/831207e0-ce1f-490a-b8f1-42e5445f86ef/advs74482-fig-0003-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/5c933749-1c32-4520-9da6-bcd9658439f9/advs74482-fig-0003-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/831207e0-ce1f-490a-b8f1-42e5445f86ef/advs74482-fig-0003-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 3<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0003&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Transfection of primary human T cells depends on LNP charge and available proteins. (a) Schematic representation of experimental workflow. T cells were activated with anti-CD3/CD28/CD2 antibodies (10&nbsp;µL/mL) and IL-2 (10&nbsp;ng/mL) for up to 10 days. On different time points following activation (i.e., day 3, 6, or 10), T cells were incubated with C12-200 or DOTAP(C12-200) lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF), IM-XF + transferrin (1&nbsp;µg/mL), and IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL), respectively. A final mRNA concentration of 1&nbsp;ng/µL for 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> T cells was used. After 24&nbsp;h incubation, transfection efficiency and cell viability were determined. (b) Transfection efficiency and (c) fold mean fluorescence intensity (fMFI) determined using flow cytometry. fMFI was calculated as MFI eGFP+ cells/MFI Luc CTR cells. (d) Cell viability was determined using a CellTiter-Glo assay after 24&nbsp;h incubation with LNPs. (e) Yield represents the percentage of viable transfected cells and was calculated by multiplying the transfection efficiency and cell viability. Bars show mean ± SD with individual donors represented by different symbols (<i>n</i> = 3, biological independent donors). Statistical analysis was performed using ordinary Two-way ANOVA with Tukey's multiple comparison test (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; <sup>****</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001). Illustration created in <a href="https://BioRender.com" class="linkBehavior">https://BioRender.com</a>.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>The optimal time point for LNP transfection of T cells after activation remains poorly defined, yet it may critically influence uptake and expression efficiency. For C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF + ApoE, T cells transfected on day 6 post-activation showed the highest transfection efficiency (54.7% ± 6.6) compared to T cells transfected on day 3 (36.3 ± 8.8%) or day 10 (23.9 ± 11.8%) post-activation (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3b</a>). However, eGFP expression levels, measured as fold mean fluorescence intensity (fMFI), were slightly higher on day 3, although not significant (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3c</a>). In contrast, DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs were less affected by activation time, showing no significant differences in transfection efficiency at different time points (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3b</a>), albeit fMFI values were clearly lower on day 10 (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3c</a>). Opposed to C12-200 LNPs, DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs also appeared to be partly cytotoxic, with remaining fractions of viable cells, as determined with CellTiter-Glo, ranging from 35.7% to 68.7%, depending on the day of transfection (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3d</a>). Hence, judging from the final yield of viable transfected cells, the combination of C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF + ApoE was identified as the most optimal transfection condition on day 3 and 6 (36.6 ± 5.7% [day 3]; 54.5 ± 6.6% [day 6]) (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3). Similar to transfection efficiency, no significant differences regarding yield were observed for DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs used in distinct transfection media (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3e</a>).</p>
            
            <p>Higher T cell binding and internalization was also observed for C12-200 LNPs in the presence of ApoE (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0004">4a</a>). Moreover, LNP-T cell interaction was highest for T cells transfected on day 6 post-activation. These results mirrored the percentage of eGFP+ cells, again correlating cell interaction with mRNA expression (Figures&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3b</a>, <a href="#advs74482-fig-0004">4a</a>). In contrast, DOTAP(C12-200) LNP binding was generally higher compared to C12-200 LNPs, although no significant difference in LNP binding was observed between different media and days of transfection (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0004">4a</a>). Nevertheless, high variation in the results was noted, most likely induced by biological donor variability. Confocal imaging supported these conclusions, with representative images shown in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0004">4b</a>. Of note, DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs seemed to be present at the cell plasma membrane, possibly via unspecific electrostatic interaction with the negatively charged cell surface, as well as inside the cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0004">4c</a>). Microscopy images further indicated that not all T cells showing a high degree of interaction with LNPs also had high mRNA expression.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0004"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/f2193786-6d70-4126-a208-92802cecefb1/advs74482-fig-0004-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/f2193786-6d70-4126-a208-92802cecefb1/advs74482-fig-0004-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/d25b08cf-41ee-43f4-99b3-2a78b7f2f9c4/advs74482-fig-0004-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/f2193786-6d70-4126-a208-92802cecefb1/advs74482-fig-0004-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 4<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0004&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">LNP interaction with primary human T cells depends on LNP composition and cell culture proteins. (a) T cells transfected in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF), IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL), or IM-XF + transferrin (1&nbsp;µg/mL) on day 3, 6, or 10 post-activation using C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) containing 1.2 mol% DiD-lipid dye at a dose of 1&nbsp;ng/µL for 5&nbsp;×&nbsp;10<sup>4</sup> T cells. Scale bar represents 20&nbsp;µm.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0190">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0190-title">3.3 Dose Increase of Non-Toxic C12-200 LNPs Improves T Cell Transfection Yield</h3>
            
            <p>As previous data showed that C12-200 LNPs did not affect cell viability (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0003">3d</a>), an mRNA dose titration was performed to further optimize T cell transfection yield. T cells at day 3 and 6 post-activation were treated with mRNA LNP doses ranging from 0.5 up to 3&nbsp;ng/µL. Despite high inter-donor variability, an increasing trend in T cell interaction was observed with mounting mRNA LNP doses (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S6</a>). A dose-dependent increase in both percentage of transfected cells and eGFP expression per cell was likewise noted, both in IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE, reaching 55.5 ± 11.6% eGFP+ cells at the highest dose tested with an fMFI of 33.2 ± 10.3 [IM-XF] (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0005">5a,b</a>). Similar to previous data, transfection on day 6 typically provided a higher fraction of eGFP+ cells, but with a lower eGFP signal per cell as compared to day 3. Interestingly, upon increased mRNA doses, the transfection advantage of adding ApoE to the cell culture medium decreased. Furthermore, even at the highest mRNA LNP dose tested, only limited cytotoxicity was observed (∼85% viable cells) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0005">5c</a>), leading to a final cell yield of ∼60% (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0005">5d</a>). Confocal microscopy confirmed the increase in T cell interaction and eGFP expression at higher mRNA doses (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0005">5e</a>). Taken together, these findings identified 3&nbsp;ng/µL as the optimal transfection condition, balancing transfection efficiency and cell viability.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0005"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/b365a817-1eac-418d-8175-204eba9c53bc/advs74482-fig-0005-m.jpg"><picture>
