                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0200">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0200-title">3.4 Kinetics of mRNA Expression Influenced by the Presence of ApoE Protein</h3>
            
            <p>Next, it was evaluated how long mRNA expression lasts in T cells upon delivery with C12-200 LNPs. Therefore, activated T cells were transfected on day 3 post-activation with C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF medium with or without ApoE (1&nbsp;µg/mL) at an mRNA dose of 3&nbsp;ng/µL. After 24&nbsp;h incubation, LNPs were removed, and cells were re-stimulated in IM-XF with anti-CD3/CD28/CD2 antibodies. A significant decrease in the percentage of eGFP+ T cells was observed from 48 to 96&nbsp;h after transfection with C12-200 LNPs, albeit transfected cells remained detectable up until 96&nbsp;h (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6a</a>). Additionally, fMFI values indicated that the intensity of eGFP expression decreased significantly after 72&nbsp;h for T cells transfected in both IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6b</a>). Nevertheless, in line with previous observations, ApoE clearly improved the extent of eGFP expression, enabling to maintain higher mRNA expression at later time points post-transfection (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6a,b</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S7</a>). Moreover, the presence of ApoE in the cell culture medium promoted T cell binding, as confirmed both quantitatively with flow cytometry as well as qualitatively on confocal microscopy images (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6c,d</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0006"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/c6e01be0-77c4-43c5-86bd-98e546af327e/advs74482-fig-0006-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/c6e01be0-77c4-43c5-86bd-98e546af327e/advs74482-fig-0006-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/6be6c6cc-c61f-4517-87d8-f2415fe21e98/advs74482-fig-0006-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/c6e01be0-77c4-43c5-86bd-98e546af327e/advs74482-fig-0006-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 6<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0006&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Kinetics of mRNA-encoded eGFP expression in C12-200 LNP-transfected primary human T cells. eGFP protein expression in CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells was determined at different time points post-transfection. Transfections were performed with DiD-labeled C12-200 lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) at a dose of 3&nbsp;ng/µL eGFP mRNA in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF) and IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL), respectively. (a) Transfection efficiency and (b) fold mean fluorescence intensity (fMFI) of eGFP expression evaluated on set time points (i.e., 24, 48, 72, 96&nbsp;h) after initial transfection. (c) LNP-T cell interaction was determined with flow cytometry and represented by the fMFI of DiD fluorescence. (d) Confocal microscopy images representing activated CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells 24&nbsp;h after transfection with DiD-labeled C12-200 LNPs at a dose of 3&nbsp;ng/µL eGFP mRNA. Signals from nuclei, DiD-LNPs, and eGFP expression are color-coded in blue, magenta, and green, respectively. Scalebar represents 20&nbsp;µm. (e) Scatterplots of CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells labeled with CD25-APC antibody on day 6 after activation. Gating was set to distinguish between FSC<sub>high</sub> and FSC<sub>low</sub> T cell populations. (f) Activation status of T cells is evaluated by the difference in forward scatter (FSC) determined during flow cytometric analysis. (g) Transfection efficiency represented as the % eGFP-expressing cells in the FSC<sub>high</sub> population. Data represent three biological independent donors (<i>n</i> = 3). Statistical analysis was performed using a mixed-effects model to account for repeated measurements (matched values), with time after transfection and transfection medium as fixed effects and donor variability as a random effect. Post-hoc comparisons between time points and between media were performed using Tukey's multiple comparisons (a,b) or Šidák's multiple comparisons test (c). Significant differences are indicated by asterisks (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; <sup>****</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001). Error bars represent mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3 biologically independent donors.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>We then aimed to examine the role of T cell activation on transfection efficiency. Previous experiments revealed that the activation marker CD25 is expressed only in cells with a high forward scatter (FSC) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6e</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S8</a>). Therefore, the transfection data were re-analyzed to distinguish between FSC<sub>high</sub> and FSC<sub>low</sub> cells as a proxy for T cell activation status (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6f</a>). Notably, eGFP expression was observed only in the FSC<sub>high</sub> cell population, indicating that only activated T cells are susceptible to C12-200 LNP transfection (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S7</a>). When solely the FSC<sub>high</sub> population was considered, most T cells were clearly transfected (90.8 ± 3.3% in IM-XF and 95.7 ± 1.3% in IM-XF + ApoE (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0006">6g</a>). Altogether, our findings indicate that T cell activation is essential for robust C12-200 LNP transfection and that supplementation of the cell culture medium with ApoE protein further stimulates both C12-200 LNP uptake and transfection, thereby extending mRNA expression.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0210">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0210-title">3.5 Ionizable and Cationic LNPs Can Efficiently Deliver nanoCAR mRNA in Activated T Cells</h3>
            
            <p>Having determined the optimal conditions for LNP transfections of CD3<sup>+</sup> T cells using an eGFP mRNA model, the neutral C12-200 and cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs were used to transfect T cells with anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA. Patients with relapsed or refractory B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphomas exhibit elevated levels of the CD20 antigen on their B cells, making CD20 CAR-T cells a plausible treatment strategy [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0054" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0054_R_d62438935e1551" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 54 - Biomedicine &amp; Pharmacotherapy">54</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0055" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0055_R_d62438935e1551" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 55 - Journal of Clinical Oncology">55</a></span>]. Moreover, both preclinical and clinical studies have shown promising results for the use of CD20 CAR-T cells in treating autoimmune diseases, such as multiple sclerosis (MS) and systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), by depleting autoreactive B cells [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0056" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0056_R_d62438935e1554" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 56 - Trends in Pharmacological Sciences">56</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0057" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0057_R_d62438935e1554" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 57 - Exploration of Immunology">57</a></span>]. NanoCAR-T cells are engineered with CAR constructs that use nanobodies as the antigen-binding domain instead of conventional single-chain variable fragments (scFvs) [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0044_R_d62438935e1557" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 44 - International Journal of Molecular Sciences">44</a></span>]. Nanobodies are derived from the V<sub>H</sub> domain of heavy-chain only antibodies (HcAbs) found in Camelidae, which lack the constant heavy 1 (CH1) domain and therefore do not have a light chain, resulting in a single antigen-binding domain and not two [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0058" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0058_R_d62438935e1563" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 58 - International Journal of Molecular Sciences">58</a></span>]. Their small size, low immunogenicity, and strict monomeric behavior are making them an attractive alternative to scFv-based CARs, with the added potential to design tandem CARs to address immune escape and relapse after CAR-T cell infusion [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0044_R_d62438935e1566" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 44 - International Journal of Molecular Sciences">44</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0058" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0058_R_d62438935e1566" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 58 - International Journal of Molecular Sciences">58</a></span>].</p>
            
            <p>Here, evaluation of the percentage of nanoCAR+ cells revealed no significant differences between IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE for the ionizable C12-200 LNPs after 24&nbsp;h. However, spiking the culture medium with ApoE allowed for a prolonged nanoCAR expression until 48&nbsp;h post-transfection, providing up to 34.3 ± 13.9% and 53.5 ± 13.5% nanoCAR+ T cells in IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE, respectively (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7a</a>). On the other hand, no significant differences could be detected between either media for cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, confirming previous findings (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7a</a>). Despite DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs providing a lower percentage of nanoCAR+ cells compared to C12-200 LNPs (i.e., 28.4 ± 9.3% versus 47.1 ± 3.1%, respectively [24&nbsp;h, IM-XF, mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3]), DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs at 1&nbsp;ng/µL still generated a higher nanoCAR expression per transfected cell than C12-200 LNPs at 3&nbsp;ng/µL (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7b</a>). Due to a clear reduction in cell viability and yield observed earlier, the dose of DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs was not further increased to 3&nbsp;ng/µL (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S9a,b</a>). Nevertheless, despite a lower cell viability 24&nbsp;h after DOTAP(C12-200) LNP treatment, the T cells exhibited similar proliferation kinetics to C12-200 LNP-treated and untreated activated T cells (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S9c</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0007"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/5eb5c190-f944-45a7-be0f-94ff0162b5b5/advs74482-fig-0007-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/5eb5c190-f944-45a7-be0f-94ff0162b5b5/advs74482-fig-0007-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/5cd2c26b-1148-41f8-b02d-f6de4054476a/advs74482-fig-0007-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/5eb5c190-f944-45a7-be0f-94ff0162b5b5/advs74482-fig-0007-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 7<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0007&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">LNP-mediated delivery of anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA allows for efficient nanoCAR expression in activated T cells. Ionizable C12-200 and cationic DOTAP(C12-200) lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) were used for the delivery of anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA in activated T cells on day 3 after initial stimulation. Experiments were performed in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF) or IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL) using a dose of 3 and 1&nbsp;ng/µL mRNA for C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, respectively. Flow cytometry was performed to determine (a) % nanoCAR+ cells in the total population, (b) fold median fluorescence intensity (fMFI) calculated as (MFI CAR+ cells—MFI Mock live cells)/(MFI CTR live cells). (c) and activation status. (d) % nanoCAR+ forward scatter high (FSC<sub>high</sub>) cells represented as the ratio of % nanoCAR+ cells in the FSC<sub>high</sub> population divided by % FSC<sub>high</sub> cells. A mixed-effects model was used for statistical analysis to account for repeated measures (matched values). Time after transfection in the specified medium and LNP treatment are described as fixed effects and donor variability as a random effect. Post-hoc comparisons between time points or between LNP treatments were performed using Tukey's (a) or Šidák's multiple comparisons test (b). Significant differences are indicated by asterisks (ns <i>p</i> &gt; 0.05; <sup>*</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.05; <sup>**</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.01; <sup>***</sup><i>p</i> &lt; 0.001; ****<i>p</i> &lt; 0.0001). Error bars represent mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3 biologically independent donors.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>Next, we again evaluated the impact of T cell activation on LNP-mediated nanoCAR expression, by gating on FSC<sub>high</sub> and FSC<sub>low</sub> cell populations. All transfection conditions achieved up to 98% FSC<sub>high</sub> T cells after 96&nbsp;h in culture (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7c</a>). Evaluation of nanoCAR expression in the FSC<sub>high</sub> subpopulation showed an increase of nanoCAR+ cells from 47.1 ± 3.1% to 63.4 ± 6.3% for C12-200 LNPs after 24&nbsp;h incubation in IM-XF (mean ± SD, <i>n</i> = 3). Contrarily, no significant increase was observed for DOTAP(C12-200) LNP-treated T cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7a,d</a>). The latter can likely be explained by the higher toxicity of DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs specifically toward unstimulated FSC<sub>low</sub> T cells (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S10b</a>). Overall, our results showed that both C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs under optimized conditions are suitable to deliver and express anti-CD20 nanoCAR in activated T cells.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="advs74482-sec-0220">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="advs74482-sec-0220-title">3.6 Transiently Engineered nanoCAR-T Cells Exhibit Tumor Cell Killing</h3>
            
            <p>The cytolytic activity of the transiently engineered nanoCAR-T cells was evaluated in an in vitro co-culture assay with CD20<sup>+</sup> Raji cells stably expressing firefly luciferase. T cells transfected with either C12-200 or DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs encapsulating anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA were harvested after 24&nbsp;h incubation in IM-XF or IM-XF + ApoE medium. Subsequently, the transfected T cell population was counted and co-cultured with Raji target cells for 24&nbsp;h at effector-to-target (E:T) ratios of 40:1, 20:1, 10:1, 5:1, and 1:1. Here, effector cells included both nanoCAR+ T cells as well as untransfected T cells. Tumor cell killing was determined by the decrease in luminescence upon cancer cell death as compared to the luminescent signal from unchallenged Raji cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0008">8a</a>) [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0059" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0059_R_d62438935e1673" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 59 - Cancers">59</a></span>]. T cells transfected with LNPs encapsulating eGFP mRNA acted as mock controls to assess unspecific lysis due to the highly active T cell state at the time of the assay (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S11</a>). Therefore, specific lysis was calculated as the cell lysis of nanoCAR-transfected T cells subtracted by the percentage cell lysis induced by mock-transfected T cells. Specific cell killing was observed for C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNP-transfected T cells in both transfection media (i.e., IM-XF and IM-XF + ApoE), with up to 40% nanoCAR-specific cell lysis (C12-200 LNP, IM-XF + ApoE, E:T 20/1) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0008">8b</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S12</a>). No significant improvement of specific tumor cell lysis was obtained when comparing nanoCAR-T cells transfected in medium with or without ApoE (<i>p</i> = 0.1835 for C12-200 LNPs and <i>p</i> = 0.6531 for DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0008">8c</a>). This result was expected for DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs as adding ApoE to the culture medium did not improve nanoCAR expression (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7a</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S13</a>). Although T cells engineered with C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF + ApoE did show an extended nanoCAR expression up to 48&nbsp;h (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0007">7a</a>), the relatively modest increase in nanoCAR expression obtained by ApoE (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S13</a>) may be insufficient to enhance tumor cell killing. Nevertheless, enhancement of transfection efficiency by ApoE appeared to be donor-dependent, as reflected by the variable percentage of nanoCAR-T cells in IM-XF vs. IM-XF + ApoE at the time of the killing assay (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0008">8d</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S13</a>). Of note, a more efficient transfection led to a higher cytolytic activity for a 10/1 E:T ratio, with a clear positive correlation between the percentage of transfected nanoCAR-T cells and specific cell lysis (<i>p</i> = 0.031) (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#advs74482-fig-0008">8e</a>; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S14</a>). However, for higher E:T ratios (e.g., 40/1), no such correlation was found (<i>p</i> = 0.75; Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S14</a>), possibly due to more non-specific killing by mock-transfected activated T cells. Likewise, the correlation for the lowest E:T ratio of 1/1 was non-significant (<i>p</i> = 0.60), as low levels of nanoCAR-T killing are likely obscured by high donor variability (Figures <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S11, S12</a>, <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S14</a>).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="advs74482-fig-0008"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/484a0282-6f5f-42f5-9882-58244e7c8277/advs74482-fig-0008-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/484a0282-6f5f-42f5-9882-58244e7c8277/advs74482-fig-0008-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/017ae79e-e2e9-4862-a359-a03111a7d0e6/advs74482-fig-0008-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/484a0282-6f5f-42f5-9882-58244e7c8277/advs74482-fig-0008-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">FIGURE 8<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=advs74482-fig-0008&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadvs.202521507" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Functional cytolytic capacities for transiently engineered CD20-specific nanoCAR-T cells. (a) Overview of experimental workflow for in vitro Raji cell co-culture assay. (b) Specific cell lysis (%) of Raji target cells expressing CD20 after 24&nbsp;h incubation with T cells expressing CD20 nanoCAR in different effector-to-target (E:T) ratios. eGFP mRNA lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) were used as a control, and a correction for background lysis was performed. Reported data represent the mean of technical triplicates ± standard error of the mean (SEM). Results are representative of two independent experiments, performed with four different donors. (c) Estimation plot showing specific lysis after engineering of nanoCAR-T cells in ImmunoCult-XF T Cell expansion medium (IM-XF) or IM-XF + apolipoprotein E (ApoE) (1&nbsp;µg/mL) for C12-200 and DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, respectively. Each dot represents an independent donor at a specified E:T ratio (i.e., 40/1, 20/1, 10/1 or 5/1), with lines connecting paired measurements. The right panel displays the mean difference with a 95% confidence interval (CI). Statistical analysis was performed using a two-tailed, paired <i>t</i>-test. (d) Scatterplots of nanoCAR+ T cells labeled with iFluor488 VHH nanobody 24&nbsp;h after transfection with C12-200 LNPs in IM-XF + ApoE (1&nbsp;µg/mL) for two independent biological donors (Donor B vs. C). Gating was set to distinguish between forward scatter high&nbsp;(FSC<sub>high</sub>) and FSC<sub>low</sub> T cell populations. (e) Correlation between % anti-CD20 nanoCAR-T cells and specific cytolytic activity (%) after 24&nbsp;h co-culture with CD20<sup>+</sup> Raji cells. A significant positive correlation was observed for E:T ratio 10/1 (<i>r = 0.54, p = 0.03, R<sup>2</sup> = 0.29</i>) when combining all treatment conditions. The solid line represents the best-fit linear regression. Data represents <i>n</i> = 4 independent biological donors. Figure created in <a href="https://BioRender.com" class="linkBehavior">https://BioRender.com</a>.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0230">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="advs74482-sec-0230-title">4 Discussion</h2>
         
         <p>It is known that proteins can electrostatically interact with specifically charged lipids depending on their isoelectric point (pI), which can be exploited for receptor-mediated cell targeting [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0039" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0039_R_d62438935e1783" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 39 - Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences">39</a></span>]. For example, the cationic DOTAP lipid is believed to promote the adsorption of proteins with a pI lower than the physiological pH of 7.4 upon intravenous administration [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0039" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0039_R_d62438935e1786" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 39 - Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences">39</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0060" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0060_R_d62438935e1786" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 60 - Nature Communications">60</a></span>]. In contrast, our ex vivo transfection results have shown that cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs transfect T cells independent from the presence of proteins with acidic pI, i.e., both ApoE (pI ∼5.6) and transferrin (pI ∼6.7). Nevertheless, it was verified that both CD71 (transferrin receptor) and LDL-R (ApoE receptor) are upregulated following T cell activation, in line with the literature [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0061" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0061_R_d62438935e1789" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 61 - In Vivo">61</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0062" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0062_R_d62438935e1789" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 62 - BMC Biology">62</a></span>]. Moreover, the transferrin receptor is involved in clathrin-mediated endocytosis (CME), and a previous study has shown improved siRNA delivery using transferrin-PEI polyplexes in activated T cells [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0063" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0063_R_d62438935e1792" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 63 - Beilstein journal of nanotechnology">63</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0065" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0065_R_d62438935e1792" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 65 - Journal of Controlled Release">65</a></span>]. Hence, alternative transfection mechanisms for DOTAP-modified LNPs might be involved, including charge-based interaction with the anionic cell membrane or cellular entry through macropinocytosis, both of which do not require specific receptor-mediated targeting [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0064" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0064_R_d62438935e1795" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 64 - Chemical Society Reviews">64</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0066" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0066_R_d62438935e1795" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 66 - ACS Applied Bio Materials">66</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0067" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0067_R_d62438935e1799" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 67 - Chemical Society Reviews">67</a></span>]. The involvement of the cationic charge is further supported by the inability of DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs to transfect Jurkat cells in the presence of serum, likely due to the positive surface charge being shielded by the random adsorption of serum proteins. Of note, it was also shown earlier that the addition of ApoE does not improve cellular uptake of cationic LNPs and that cationic DOTAP-modified LNPs predominantly acquire other serum proteins with acidic pI (i.e., vitronectin) on their surface when injected in the blood circulation [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0038" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0038_R_d62438935e1802" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 38 - Journal of Controlled Release">38</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0040_R_d62438935e1802" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 40 - Molecular Therapy">40</a></span>]. Both confocal microscopy and flow cytometry confirmed a more intense interaction between DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs and T cells compared to C12-200 LNPs, which could explain both the markedly higher mRNA expression per cell as well as reduced cell viability observed for the cationic LNPs. Previous research has indicated that the concentration and type of cationic lipid used in lipid-based formulations may have a profound impact on cytotoxicity [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0068" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0068_R_d62438935e1805" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 68 - Nanomaterials">68</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0070" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0070_R_d62438935e1805" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 70 - ACS Nano">70</a></span>].</p>
         
         <p>Opposed to cationic LNPs, ApoE is known to interact with the surface of charge-neutral ionizable LNPs (e.g., DLin-KC2-DMA LNPs) and promote transfection efficiency via LDL-R mediated endocytosis and improved cytosolic delivery [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0040_R_d62438935e1811" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 40 - Molecular Therapy">40</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0046" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0046_R_d62438935e1811" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 46 - Journal of Controlled Release">46</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0071" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0071_R_d62438935e1814" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 71 - ACS Nano">71</a></span>]. Literature also shows that the presence of DOPE helper lipids in the C12-200 LNPs contributes to a strong ApoE-LNP interaction [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0072" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0072_R_d62438935e1817" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 72 - Biomaterials Science">72</a></span>]. Here, we likewise demonstrated that ionizable C12-200 LNPs generally require the presence of ApoE for improved T cell transfection, which may be driven by enhanced LDL-R expression upon T cell activation [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0061" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0061_R_d62438935e1820" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 61 - In Vivo">61</a></span>]. Nevertheless, additional experiments would be required to validate LDL-R involvement. Consequently, the presence of an activation step in the manufacturing workflow, as well as the time post-activation are of crucial importance to obtain an optimal transfection efficiency. In contrast, the timing of transfection appears less important for cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs, again supporting a passive charge-based rather than an active receptor-mediated transfection mechanism. The ability to transfect less activated T cell phenotypes could hold a therapeutic advantage. Studies have shown that a shorter ex vivo culture period is associated with improved CAR T cell functionality by providing less differentiated T cells and reducing the risk of T cell exhaustion [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0041" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0041_R_d62438935e1823" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 41 - Frontiers in Immunology">41</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0073" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0073_R_d62438935e1823" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 73 - Journal for ImmunoTherapy of Cancer">73</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0075" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0075_R_d62438935e1823" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 75 - The Lancet Haematology">75</a></span>]. Hence, such an accelerated manufacturing workflow can substantially reduce the treatment cost and vein-to-vein time, thus increasing treatment accessibility and avoiding disease progression in critically ill patients prior to receiving the CAR-T cell product [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0043" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0043_R_d62438935e1827" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 43 - Cell and Gene Therapy Insights">43</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0075" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0075_R_d62438935e1827" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 75 - The Lancet Haematology">75</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0076" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0076_R_d62438935e1830" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 76 - Human Gene Therapy Methods">76</a></span>]. Of note, activation of the T cells does remain necessary, as our results suggest no transfection of non-proliferating T cells.</p>
         
         <p>Anti-CD20 nanoCAR-T cells were generated upon treatment with both charge-neutral and cationic LNP formulations. Depending on the transfection conditions used, nanoCAR expression could be detected up to 72&nbsp;h after transfection for C12-200-treated T cells in the presence of ApoE. The high variability in mRNA expression kinetics observed between different biological donors is likely due to differences in T cell proliferation rates and metabolic activity. Specifically, donor T cells with a high proliferation rate exhibit short-lived CAR expression, as both the mRNA and the expressed CAR are rapidly diluted upon cell division [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0077" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0077_R_d62438935e1836" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 77 - Journal of Biological Chemistry">77</a></span>]. As the proliferative capacity is highly donor dependent, this will be an important factor to consider for effective transient cell therapies [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0078" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0078_R_d62438935e1839" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 78 - Biomarker Research">78</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0079" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0079_R_d62438935e1839" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 79 - Experimental Hematology &amp; Oncology">79</a></span>]. Additionally, a higher initial mRNA expression per cell will prolong the persistence of mRNA expression. Previous studies have reported varying durations of mRNA-mediated CAR expression following LNP delivery, with expression persisting from approximately 48&nbsp;h up to 7 days post-transfection [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0004" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0004_R_d62438935e1842" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4 - Advanced Healthcare Materials">4</a>, <a href="#advs74482-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0024_R_d62438935e1842" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Molecular Therapy Methods &amp; Clinical Development">24</a></span>, <span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0080" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0080_R_d62438935e1845" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 80 - Small">80</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0082" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0082_R_d62438935e1845" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 82 - Science">82</a></span>]. However, the critical time threshold required to achieve effective therapeutic outcomes has yet to be defined. One potential strategy to extend mRNA-based CAR expression involves reducing CAR-T cell proliferation upon transfection. This would result in more gradual mRNA dilution as a result of lower cell division rates, thereby prolonging CAR expression [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0024_R_d62438935e1848" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Molecular Therapy Methods &amp; Clinical Development">24</a></span>]. In addition, the incorporation of self-amplifying mRNA or circular mRNA could further extend mRNA expression [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0083" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0083_R_d62438935e1852" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 83 - Advanced Drug Delivery Reviews">83</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0085" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0085_R_d62438935e1852" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 85 - Nature Communications">85</a></span>]. These approaches maintain the inherent benefits of mRNA while optimizing expression levels and CAR persistence to support clinically effective CAR-T cell therapies.</p>
         
         <p>In addition to CAR expression on the T cell membrane, the potency of engineered T cells is of major therapeutic importance. Our results showed that both the ionizable C12-200 and cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs allow for the engineering of transient anti-CD20 nanoCAR-T cells and induce effective cancer cell killing. Of note, substantial background killing was observed for mock-transfected T cells, a phenomenon that is often not considered in the literature. As our results underscored the importance of T cell stimulation for LNP-mediated transfection, the T cells may have been in a highly proliferative state during the potency assay. This could have facilitated secretion of cytotoxic molecules such as granzymes and perforins, potentially contributing to antigen-independent tumor cell killing [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0086" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0086_R_d62438935e1858" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 86 - Nature Cancer">86</a>-<a href="#advs74482-bib-0089" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0089_R_d62438935e1858" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 89 - British Journal of Cancer">89</a></span>]. Moreover, chronic stimulation may upregulate NK receptors on the T cell surface, which in turn could promote innate-like, antigen-independent responses [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0090" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0090_R_d62438935e1861" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 90 - Trends in Immunology">90</a></span>]. Equally important is the influence of donor variability on nanoCAR expression and consequently, specific cell killing capacity. Overall, the presence of ApoE in the serum-free cell culture medium improves nanoCAR expression over time for C12-200 LNPs. Nevertheless, in some biological donors, transfection efficiency appeared less affected by ApoE, which might be caused by variability in T cell activation status during transfection. Additionally, previous research has demonstrated a positive correlation between T cell activation and LDL-R upregulation [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0091" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0091_R_d62438935e1864" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 91 - Journal of Cell Biology">91</a></span>]. Another study revealed that the percentage of LDL-R<sup>+</sup> T cells was directly proportional to the percentage of transduced T cells upon activation, suggesting that LDL-R expression can serve as a predictive marker for viral transduction efficiency [<span><a href="#advs74482-bib-0061" class="bibLink tab-link" id="advs74482-bib-0061_R_d62438935e1869" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 61 - In Vivo">61</a></span>]. Hence, further investigation into the potential correlation between LDL-R expression in activated T cells and the distinction between responders and non-responders to ApoE-enhanced transfection would provide valuable insights for future adoptive T cell engineering. Finally, our DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs allowed for the engineering of functional nanoCAR-T cells, even though lower metabolic activity was observed 24 h after LNP treatment. Nevertheless, a deeper understanding of the long-term effects of cationic lipid-mediated cytotoxicity on CAR-T cell functionality will be essential for advancing their clinical translation.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0240">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="advs74482-sec-0240-title">5 Conclusion</h2>
         
         <p>This study demonstrates that LNP charge, cell culture medium, and T cell activation state have a profound impact on primary T cell transfection. Specifically, charge-neutral ionizable C12-200 LNPs showed improved transfection efficiency in the presence of recombinant ApoE protein. In contrast, our data indicates that cationic DOTAP(C12-200) LNPs rather transfect T cells in a charge-dependent manner and do not rely on specific proteins in the culture medium or T cell-specific receptor expression. Overall, the use of non-viral LNPs for ex vivo T cell engineering could represent a cost-effective and time-efficient alternative to current viral-based manufacturing methods. Here, cationic LNPs offer the overall benefit of both reducing cell culture medium complexity and enabling efficient transfection across a broader T cell activation window.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="advs74482-sec-0250">

L.H. formulated and characterized the lipid nanoparticles, performed T cell isolation, transfection experiments, functional T cell assays, and data analysis. S.D.M. assisted with the&nbsp;in vitro&nbsp;cytolytic activity assay. S.D.M, J.I., and B.V. provided anti-CD20 nanoCAR mRNA. D.B. and M.D.V. assisted with T cell isolation. M.D.V. contributed to flow cytometry analysis. L.H. wrote the article and prepared the figures. All authors contributed to manuscript reviewing and editing.</p>
         </section>

            Citation manager file format
        </h3>
        <p class="citation-modal__tips-text citation-modal__cycleElement" tabindex="0">

You can then copy the formatted citation (as displayed) or download it as file, to your device. If the RefWorks format is chosen, the 'Download' button will be replaced with an option to directly export to RefWorks
        </p>
